=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.98)
2015 windsor Ave.,
Waco, Texas,
May 20, 1941
Hon. Tom Connall,
U. S. Senate,
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Connally:
For the purrece of mare clearly ilustretine my peint,
there is enclosee nerenitn eaiteri fram ty Wace Reeore of
Friday, May l6, 1941. I mave lonr been wondering way we in
this country should be girding ourselves ana spending billions
for National Defense on one hand against the Axis powers, and
then on the other hand allowing the war machines of those same
Axis powers to be supplied with oil, scrap iron and other products
for their war machines, all because sone selfish interests in the
country are greedy for profits. How long must such continue?
I am one humdred per cent for the strongest possible National
Defense and believe we really should have had universal military
training since 1920, but I would 1ike to ask one question, that
is, if our National Congress can enact a Selective Service Law
requiring our boys to serve in the Army at s21.00 per month, then
by the same token why can't tey enact laws that will plug the
loop noles and prevent selfish interests from furnishing war
supplies to our potential enemies?
This letter is not written just to be critical, but be-
cause I am vitally interested and seriously alarmed. I understand
that the shipment of scrap iron has now been curtailed but last
year I saw in our own Texas ports whole mountains of scrap iron
being loaded on foreign ships and oil tankers shuttling in and out
like a bunch of bees - all bound for those countries who are eager
to turn their war machines on us the moment they feel strong enough.
For more than two years during the last war I served my
country here and abroad in the battle fields of France and I am
willing to do everything I am physically able to do again, but
can't we wake up before it is too late and say in no uncertain
terms to Capital, Labor and all others that they, as well as the
soldiers, must contribute something and sacrifice something? I
now have a boy 18 years old but when he goes to train for fighting
I would hate to think that the very country training him was also
supplying war material to those who would shoot him.
Very truly yours,
(Signed) R. H. Claypool
R. H. Claypool

My dear Senator:
I cannot understand the attitude of Congress
or the Adniniatration in our veal ings with Janan,
the Administration nas riven cuina creaits of
hundreds of millions of dollars to help them
defeat Japan and then turn around sell Japan
millions of bbls. of fuel cil to enable Janan to
continue the fight.
The enclosed clipping was taken from the
Los Angeles Times of this ate (May ?4) and a
gentleman employed by a west coast shiuping con-
cern informs me tnat Jap boats are continuely
going out with nundreds of tnousands of (1oo gal)
bbls. of oil marked Motor Products but taken from
the same stack that English boats load from which
is marked Aviation Gasoline.
This does not make sense to Tax-payers, who
would like to see more action and less talk.
Yours very respectfully,
(S1gned) H. F. McGinley
H. F. McGinley

Dear Sir:
Last fall the Young homen's Christian Association of Chicago had many
discussions on international relations and the foreign policy of the
United States. At the conclusion of these discussions a poll of
opinion was conducted among the membership and two hundred and eighty-
five members voted. A substantial majority of our membership who voted
in this poll favored a complete embargo on war materials to Japan on
the greund that Japan is the aggressor in the Far East. Our member-
ship represents a reasonably gooa crcss section of the women in Chicago
and for that reason we believe tlis vote to be significant. At a mem-
bership mecting the Associ: ticn ther adopted an embergo on war materials
to Japan as one item in a minimum prograr of action for the current year.
Recently this subject has been under discussion in our Metropolitan Board
of Directors and at their meeting last Friday they voted unanimously to
urge upon you the prohibition of all exports of war materials to Japan.
The discussion whioh preceded this vote indicated that the members have
considerable appreciation of the complexity of the problem and the delicacy
of our Far Eastern relations. Nevertheless, they believe that the present
controls of exports to Japan are insufficient.
Very truly yours,
  B
JUN21941
Lilace Reid Barnes,
FILED
President.

Department of State
As a loyal Afferiosmoyho is a stockholder in
Socony -Vacuum Oil, I have expostulated with the pres-
ident of the company, Mr. Brown, against exporting
oil to Japan, a cruel invader and a potential enemy.
He replied that his co. is engaged in internation
trade; that if this country wants the trade stopped, it
must be done by the State Dep't.
Stop this trade. Are believers in it honest when
they say that if Japan cannot bet oil from us they will
prevent our getting rubber from the East Indies?
Where is the famed American inventiveness? Cannet our
chemists fabricate rubber? Germans have.
The Germans sneer that Democracies destroy themselves.
We are destroying ourselves with this trade in oih
I, a poor woman, whostlargest stock-holding is in Socony,
bef you to stop this oil trade.
Yours truly,
(niis/ Ang N. Dour

Dear Sir:
I have your letter and the letter from the
Department of State on my recent inquiry to you concerning
the shinments of oil to Japan. Very interesting letters
on licensing and so forth, but it does not answer the
question why are we shippin oil and petroleum to Japan.
I call your attention to a news item that
appeared on May lcth, a statenent issued by Mal iki of
the Honolulu Oil Company, wno said, "When the State
Department ordered, oil supilies to Japan would be
stopped."
I believe if we are goin to take a stand
on this vosition, we nad better not adopt an "Easement
Policy" to Japan by givin; them cil as a bribe. This
begins to look too much like a Munich deal.
Very truly yours,
(Signed)
Jack J. Lande

ranklin D. Roosevelt

894.24/1425
1striet of cotambia
Dear Mr. President:
AW
I voted for you three times. .
I have been in favor of our helping people of
every nation who were willing to fight for Freedom.
I listened to your recent broadcast with
wholehearted approval. It thrilled and inspired me.
BUT, when I read the report of your press
conference, the following day, I could hardly believe
my eyes. What a letdown! You seem to be un-saying the
best that you had previously said in your radio speech.
You have it in your power to stop these
Communistic CIO strikes. In you radio discourse, you
said they had to stop. In your press conference, you
made it clear that you were not going to take measures
44.24
to stop them.
These men who refuse to work at building ships,
planes, and guns, are already being paid more for an
hour's work than a soldier-boy gets for a day!s work.
FILED
If anybody strikes, it should be the soldier. Yet, for
S
this, he would be courtmartialed and imprisoned.
/HH
However, I began this letter to write you
about something else. It is the enclosed clipping -
The Oil Enigma - from the editorial page of our Miami
Daily Herald.
Just as we were about to buy a thousand dollar's
worth of Defense Bonds, we picked up the paper and read
the above editorial. It is confusing in the extreme. In
2

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
His Excellency Franklin D. Roosevelt
Page #2
fact, it is very disturbing. While it is hard to believe,
still it must be true. I told my wife that we certainly
would not buy any Defense Bonds, if this is the way things
are being handled in Washington.
Here, we are being taxed to pay for war goods to
be sent to China. Then, you deliberately allow oil and gas
to be shipped to Japan to drive the planes for the dastardly
Japs to blowup the Chinese!
Now, Mr. President, you cannot answer the
hundreds of thousands of letters which are constantly being
written to you. But, may I ask that you please be good
enough to pess on this letter to one of your many secretaries.
Ask him to simply write on the bottom of this letter whether
or not the contents of this clipping are true or false. Then,
return it to me in the enclosed self-addressed envelope.
Thank you.
Sincerely,
TH:mf
TOM HUSTON
Enclosure
cc - Senator Claude Pepper
cc - Editor John Knight, Miami Herald

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.97)
Tom Hustón
MIAMI.FLORIDA
104
Mr. President:
Cinee vritiny the eneloced Tetter, T
have read reporte that our rmy m Nvy re le
trained flyers for service in the Chinese Army.
Does this mean that our boys, flying our
bombers, must face Japenese guns made of American steel
borne by Japanese planes driven with American gasoline?
I want to be told -- yes or no.
Ah, the fumbling and stupid democracies.
Little wonder that Hitler knocks them down as fast as
they stand up before him.
But, what can we average citizens do?
We can go to the polls. We can vote into our public
offices the best brains offered us. After that, I
know of nothing left to do except to raise Cain ebout
the apparent inefficiency which often follows.
We can sing GOD BLESS AMERICA until we
are blue in the face. But we know that, since the-
dawn of time, God has allowed the weak to be annihilated
by the strong.
1020
Unless we quit pussyfooting, and stand up
and fight with all the might within us, we are going
down just as the weak have always gone.
Sincerely,
18
TH:mf
TOM HUSTON

THESEORETARY
Dear Mr. Secretary:
Our reports show that for the last six months exports
from the United States to Japan of petroleum products, including
crude petroleum, gasoline, naptha, kerosene, lubricating oil, and
fuel oil, were as follows:
October..
.1,732,456 bbls.
November.
.2,909,214 bbls.
894.24/1428
December.
.1,744,308 bble.
January.
.1,666,355 bbls.
February.
.1,339,113 bbls.
March.
.1,433,535 bbls.
Apparently, all of these shipments were outside the
export license controls which, up to the present moment, appear
to cover only aviation gas of a high octane content or capable
of being transformed into aviation gas of high octane content.
The following report of the President's press confer-
ence of May 2nd may be of interest in this connection:
"In reply to a question on persistent reports
2
of U. S. shipments of oil and gasoline to Japan,
the President cautioned that the shipments so
made were of types not banned by existing
licensing or embargo laws. Commerce and State
Department figures, he said, showed that the
gasoline shipped to Japan was not classified
i:
as "aviation" gasoline, having a lower octane
(.)
rating."
2
1941
Sincerely yours,
PS/MFM

Dear Secretary:
The people of the Washington Coast are
still bewildered as they see thousands of tons of scrap
iron, copper bars and millions of gallons of gasoline
being shipped to Japan, a large part of which is undoubt-
19 %24
edly being transhipped to Germany. Also we note that
fourteen + housand tons of wheat are being shipped from
Canadian Ports to Vladivostok by Japanese freighters,
undoubtedly destined to feed German soldiers.
It is bewildering to the people of this
Coast to see this procedure under our ose, when the
administration is talking loudly about all-out aid to
Brittain, and when our own future in the Pacific is
undoubtedly menaced by the building up of a Japanese
war machine.
It causes many of us to wonder if there
is one ounce of patriotism or honor in the munition
trade, where a dollar of profit can be gained.
Very truly yours.
N194
FILEL
Fred A.Smith

Tokyo's despatch 5588, May 12, 1941,

This despatch transmits data of exports
from NEI to Japan furnished confidentially
by the Dutch Legation at Tokyo taken from a
report from the NEI Government to the home
Government recommending restrictions on NEI
shipments in l94l to Japan of important
articles. These shipments in 1940 showed
sharp advances over l939 and it is possible
that moderate quantities were shipped to
Germany. The NEI authorities are trying to
prevent such shipments to Germany by scaling
Japan's quotas down to her own estimated
requirements. The NEI and Malaya authorities
have an understanding as to orders from
Japanese purchasers ospecially for goods pro-
duced in both areas.

discussed with the Comptroller for Restrioted

whereby exports from either Malaya or the N.E.I.

to what 1s estimated as normal requirements.

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.99)
P
Y
Honorable Andrew J. May,
Chairman, Committee on Military Affairs,
House of Representatives.
Dear Mr. May:
The War Department does not favor the onactment of H. J.
Res. 18y, "Requesting the Presid nt of the United States of America
to exercise the powers vested in him under section 6 of Public No.
703, 76th Congress, to the end that no further shipments of
petroleum or petroleum products shall be made to Japan, and for
other purposes."
The effect of this joint resolution would be to provide
by law that whenever the President shall issue a proclamatien under
authority of Seetion 6 of tho Act of July 2, 1940 (Public No. 703,
76th Congress), prohibiting or curtailing the expertation of any
military equipment or munitions, or component parts thereof, the
exportation of petroleum and petroleum products to Japan shall be
deemed subject to the prohibitions or curtailment sc imposed.
To the extent indicated this statutory mandate would
supersede the discretionary power now vested in the President by
Seetion 6 of the Act of July 2, 1940.
There can be no question that petroleum and petroloum pro-
ducts constitute vital national defense resources mhich should be
conserved even though no present shortage of those produets may
exist. Hewever, prohibition or curtailment of the expertatien ef
these products to a particular country may result in international
cemplications far reaching in consequence. Whether or not such
action should be taken necessarily depends on national policy which
may be affected from time to time by possible changes in world een-
ditiens. The War Department therefore believes that it would be im
the natienal interest to continue to leave such questions to the
diseretion of the President.
For the foregoing reasons the War Department recemmends

=== Page 123 === (confidence 0.98)
that H. J. Res. 189 be not onacted into law.
Sincerely yours,
Secretary of War.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

June 7, 1941.
H：
Reference that part of the Department's
telegram no. 219, June 5, 6 p.m. to the
Amerioan Consul at Manila permitting the
issuance of licen es, without restriction as
to quantity, covering exports from the Philip-
pines of iron ore.
The normal exports of iron ore from the
Philippines to Japan (which is understood to
be practically the sole consumer) amounts to
600,000 tons. In 1940 the exports of iron
ore from the Philippines to Japan amounted to
approximately 1,220,000 tons valued at about
$2,800,000. Exports of iron ore from the
Philippines to Japan ran in the first four
months of 194l about one-third higher than in
the same period during 1940. The foregoing
quantities are not large in terms of iron and
steel production.
If the United States purchased the iron
ore it would remain in the Philippines because
of lack of shipping tonnage. If the United
States stopped all production of iron ore in
the Philippines by refusing to buy production
and by refusing to allow its exportation to
Japan, the result would be that a number of
people would be thrown out of employment.
It is

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
It is expected that within the next month
a direotive will be issued allowine normul
pre-war quantities of iron ore to o to Jupan.
In the meantine, it is felt that existing
limitations on shipping facilities will not
permit dangerous quantities of iron ore to
reach Japan from the Philippines.

imdustries were built upon the availability of domestic
iron ores, Japan had no substantial domestic deposits of

ese iron and steei industry was developed in a manner
which had to take this peculiarity into account; and to
grow up on a scra base.
In time of peace this was no disadvantage since it
enauled to be brought about a rapid expansion in steel
mapacity in an economical and efficient manner. Production
capacity consisted of open hearth furnaces in which rela-
tively cheap scrap of varying quality could be re-melted
with varying quantities of pig iron. Much of the pig
iron smelted in this way originated in the blast furnaces
in Japan though a very substantial part of the ore from
which it was derived was of foreign origin and though Japan
furthermore had an appreciable import of pig iron before
thewar.
In time of war, of course, Japan's iron and steel in-
dustry is exceedingly ill-balanced for it can easily be
out off from many of its prinoipal souroes of supply of
sorap, imported pig iron and iron ore.
The production of pig iron in Japan has been expanded
as the ingot steel production was expanded but in the last
few years Japan has not been able to inorease the make of
pig irom produced in the country in proportiom to the
production of steel. Indeed, in 1940 it is reliably re-
ported that Japanese produotion of pig iron fell below 1939
production though at the same time inget steel output showed

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.97)
IBON ORE EPORTS TO JAPAM
In assessing the importance of exports of
iron ore to Japan it is necessury to take into ccount
all the raw material supplies for the Japunese iron nd
steel industry and to determine the relative restriction
on output which can be imposed by their curtailment.
Table  in tn ttached Memorundum shows that
some So% of the raw materi ls of tn Japanese iron and
steel industry are normally imported from sources outside
Japanese control and that this figure is mace up of iron
ore 21.5%, pig iron 7.5%, scrap steel 26%. The embargo
on exports of scrap to Japan from the United States und the
British Empire and the  eduction, by about two thirds,
of the normal exports of pi iron from India, has alrely
resulted in a curtailment, by some 30%, or the total
supplies and this should probably be raised to 33% ir,
as is believed, present United States exports of pig irou
are small.
The remaining item of iron ore is divided be-
tween Malaya and the Philippines, Japanese imports from
these two sources representing some 14.5% and 7% res-
pectively of total iron and steel industry raw materials
on the 1937/1939 verage, (though the 1940 figures would
be slightly higher). In terms of tonnages, Malayan
exports averaged 1,600,000 tons in 1936/1938 rising to
1,900,000 tons in 1939/1940, to which latter figure they
are now limited. Philippine exports averaged 700,000 tons
in 1956/1938 r1sing to some 1,200,000 tons in 1959/1940.
While it is evident that some reduotion on
these figures would be desirable with the objeet of still
/trther

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.98)
2
further curtailin the raw material supplies of the
Japanese iron an steel industry, ccount must be ta.en
as to whether the repercussions resulting from sucn re-
duction of exports might be out of proportion to the effect
produced in Japanese industry.
In the ease of Malaya, the mines tre owned and
entirely run by the Japmese and are for the most part
situated in Trengann which is a poor state where export
duties on the ore form an important part of the revenue.
The imposition of restrictions is therefore Likely to be
regarded by Japan as unjustified and provocative and may
also create local financial and economic problems. Similar
considorations apply in the Philippines (though recent in-
creases in production have been proportion tely much higher
there). In neither territory is there any alternative out-
let or any case for restriction on supply grounds.
On the other hand, a reduction of say 50% in the
exports from both territories would only effect n added
113 reduction in Japanese total supplies.
In view of these considerations, it appe.rs, on
balance, to be unnecessary to cause the local dislocation which
would be likely to follow a curtailment of iron ore exports.
Insofar, however, as it may be found pessible to restrict
Philippine exports to a figure more closely approaching that
of the years immediately preceding 1939, assurance can be
given that such reduction will not be allowed to be refleoted
in any increase in exports from Malaya. 
Ome further important ecnsideration needs to be
taken iato aceount. It has been the policy of His Majesty's
Gevermmeat in considering the steel industry of Japem to

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.95)
pay greater attention to reducin the effective use of
that industry for armament purposes by curtailing supplies
of manganese, chrome, tun sten, vunadium, nickel, cobalt
and molybdenum and they nave therefore concentrated more
on restriction of supplies of these materials than on those
of iron ore. Similarly, in the c se of the Philippines, a
severe curtailment of supplies to Japan of chrome and man-
ganese ore might well be of more value in restricting the
Japanese armament industry without inflicting great hardship
on the territory, than a corresponding restriction on exports
of iron ore.

THE SUPPLY POSITION IN THE IRON AND
STEEL INDUSTRMIN JAPAN
The British Embassy has given us a very interesting
memorandum under the above heading, from which it appears
FW
that Japanese efforts to increase production of steel
ingots and castings have not been notably successful al-
894.24/1454
though production has increased from 2,253,000 metric
tons in 1930 to 6,414,000 metric tons in 1939 and an est1-
mated 6,455,000 tons in 1940. Production of pig iron 1s
reported to have increased only very moderately, from
2,415,000 metr1c tons 1n 1934 to 3,310,000 in 1939 and 1s
estimated to have fallen off to 3,000,000 metric tons in
1940. Use of sorap iron in the production of ingot steel
and steel castings appears to have increased substantially
since 1934. Imports of scrap in the f1ve years, 1934-1939,
have exceeded apparent consumption by 1-1/3 million tons
which may represent the amount of scrap reserves. (It is
believed that consumption of scrap has substantially ex-

According to the Pritieh memorandum, raw material
for production of cast iron and steel was imported to the
extent of 55.2 percent in 1937-1939 (three-year average)
while domestic sources suppl1ed 44.& percent. In 1938,
when production of steel ingot and castings was 5,87?,000
metric tons, only 49.7 percent of the raw material supplied
was imported but in 1939, when production increased to
6,414,000 metr1c tons, domestic sources were apparently
unable to supply the additional raw material required and
1mported material made up 53.6 percent of the total. The
increased demand for imported material was apparently met
largely by drawing on the reserve of imported scrap, as
imports cf scrap were only 100,000 tons greater than in
1938 wh1le an increase of 300,000 tons in the iron content
of imported ore was offset by a decrease of 335,000 tons
in imports of pig iron.

be reduced to 5,667,000 tons, of which the iron content of

ply. A complete embargo on pig iron from India would ac-
count for an additional 6 percent. These percentages are,
of course, based on the assumption that the total supply
1s reduced by the amount of the reduction in imported
materials. It is possible that the Japanese have been
able to increase their domestic production or their 1m-
ports from Manchuria and China through exploitation of low-
grade iron sands and low-grade ores but it 1s believed
likely that a curtailment in imports from British and Ameri-
can sources 1s fairly directly reflected in the Japanese
supply.

According to Table 1 appended to the British memo-
randum, Japanese importe of iron ore from Nalaya increased

62 percent Fe in which case the iron content of ore
imported from the Fhilippines would be about 565,000
metric tons 1n 1938 and 655,000 metric tons in 1939.) In
194o Philippine exports of ore to Japan amounted to about
1,225,000 tons of ore with an iron content of about
760,000 tons. There is no information as to exports from
British Malaya in 1940. Assuming that they were about the
same as in 1939, a reduction in shipments to the 1938
level, which would appear not unreasonable, would represent
a decline of about 446,000 tons in the supply of 1ron to
Japan or about 8 percent of the 1939 supply minus the
American scrap. (Shipments from the Philippines in the
first half of 1941 were at the rate of about 1,400,000
tons of ore for the year, while shipments from Malaya have
also probably 1ncreased since 1939.)

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
It le believed that uen a curtailment of exports
(to the 1938 level) could not result in economic dis-
locations in either Malaya or the Philippines. The state
of Trengganu could probably make up the loss in revenue
from export duties by increasing those dutles. A suff1-
ciently sharp increace in the rates might have the doubly
desirable effect of sustaining the revenue while at the
same time discouraging exports. A curtailment of 446,000
tons in the supply of raw material to the Japanese iron
and steel industry would not represent a vital blow to
that industry but, taken together with the curtailment
of the supply of scrap iron from the United States, would
certainly make a considerable difference in the amount of
iron and steel available for war materials. A curtail-
ment of pig iron exports from India to Japan would also
have some effect.

Cameron, Texas,
June 9, 1941.
Hon. Tom C naelly.
Washinrton. D C
Dear Mr. Connally:
You may remember me as the daughter of the late C. B.
Wade of Temple and Belton.
I do not represent any comnittee, roup, or organize-
tion of any kind but am writing yu for some information
that may be par sed n to many Tevas women who ere in e
dezed condition es to our nationel affairs.
As you know I am just an average Texas woman. I am
a college graduate, have served the public for twenty years
half of that time on a college staff. I served under Civil
Service in the last war. I am e D.A.R., a U.D.C., I work
in club, church, Ped Cross, and bridge circles. Everywhere
I go the women are asking guestions and wondering about
things that none can answer. We try to keep as well in-
formed as possible daily. We lieten to the radio, read
our state papere, and keep up with all the best magazines.
We hear out national figures lecture when they are in our
state. The head lines scream with the radio that the people
shou'd wake up and we feel that we are fully ware of our
serious etate of affairs. We are ewed at the delay in
Washington. We agree with you and eppreciate your stand
on delayed defense.
We ere told that individuals, companiee, and corpora-
tions in the United States are selling war materials to
Japan and Germany and to other nations who bootleg these
materials to Japan and Germany. They no doubt are making
their millione. Then the cry comee to us, the people, to
feed, clothe, and heal the nations who are the victime of
war. We are arming outeelves, sending our men, and working
with all the different organizations to relieve conditions
brought on us by goods supplied from the United States.
At the same time we are building up defense against Germany
and Japan we are feeding them their war needs. We are told
that taxes will be burdensome and that we must lower our
standards of living and then our own country is helping to
bring this condition about. All of this adds up to great
confusion in a woman's logic. All of our women are ready
and glad to do anything that our country asks or needs of
us but we do not underetand the "why" of things.

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
I know you are a very buay man but I hope you have a
secretary with enough time to explain some things that will
te passed on to many Texas women. We are alert and awake
to the situation but are powerless to do anything. We
wonder why so much delay in action in Washington.
Sincerely,
Any Wade

Te note that our country is exporting large quantities of
Cil to apan. Thile our corporation has never done business
in the past with Japan, we have been approached by Mitsui &
Company, who has requested us to make quotations on Philip-
pine Chrome Ore and Peruvian Tungsten Ure, F.O.B. Philippine

Licensing Act, and I wanted to know, Why, it wae not en-

when thought detrimental to Netional Defense and that 1s
exactly what I contended in my letter, selling oil, to
Japan 1s building the war suppliee of a Nation that it
is more than probable we will sooner or later have to fight.

jubilant, we thought, at last, our friende the Chinese
were going to get a break.
Now we find regardless of the Sec'ys long and con-
siderete explamation, that the Act wee only a camouflage,
oil 1s still going to Japan boat after boat, and when the
Sec'y is asked wHY, he does not answer and the taxpayer

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.98)
OIL PRODUCERS AGENCY
OF CALIFORNIA
1O35 SUBWAY TERMINAL BUILDING
LOS ANGELES, CALIFORNIA
MICHIGAN 1891
une 7. 194
CALIFORNIA
WEEKLY
PETROLEUM
BULLET IN
NC. 1941-25
WEEK ENDED JUNE 21, 1941
CRUDE PRODUOTICN dropped off Frem the record igh mark set weelt before
last - state's output 1ast veek was 639,600 B/2 (A.P.1.), down 10, 400 B/D.
The decrease in production last week coincided with the established trend
of higher rate of output during first half of each month followed by curtailed
production during latter half. However, the ratio of June production during the
first 24 days to that of May l94l has remained almost constant, according to data
from the Administrator. The average for June 1-24, inc., was 642, 300 B/D - up
10,400 B/D over the previous month during same time. Indications at present are
that the average for the whole month of June will be close to 635, 000 B/D - highest
monthly output since December 1938.
RUINS TO STILLS increased 25, 900 B/D over the previous week as last week's
average stepped up to 572.700 B/D (A.P.I.) - very close to the reccrd set in the
week of June 7. Thus far in 1941 (January 1-June 21, inc.) crude has gone to
stills at rate of 511,800 B/D as against 493,300 B/D at same time in 1940 - and
481,700 B/D in 1939.
EXPORTS OF ALL PRODUCTS (tanker shipments) fell off to 133, 500 B/D - down
32, 300 B/D from the week before. Exports to JAPAN which were at a fairly high
level in the preceding week dropped to 40,800 B/D - decrease of 33, 600 B/D.
Shipments to OTHER PACIFIC FOREIGN ports were likewise lower - average last week
was 57,200 B/D - down 34,200 B/D from the week before. Offsetting these decreases,
there were 35,500 B/D shipped to the EAST COAST last week compared with no ship-
ments in the preceding week.
So far this month (1-21, inc.) exports have averaged 144.000 B/D - about
12,000 B/D over the same period in May 1941 - but 24. 500 B/D under shipments in
June 1940. Exports to JAPAN which averaged 71,800 B/D in June 1940, amount to
only 48.400 B/D in the current month. In May 194l exports to the Japanese were
49,500 B/D as of the 23rd, but increased takings in the closing days of last month
pushed the average for the entire month up to 68, 500 B/D. It is quite possible
that the same thing may happen in the last days of this month but the increase will
have to be larger than last month's in order to equal the June 1940 movement.
Shipments to OTHER PACIFIC FOREIGN ports so far in June/Hightly ahead of those
last month. Total movement to Pacific points, other than Japan, stands at 70,500
B/D up to the 2lst of this month - during same period in May 1941 average was
66.000 B/D - in June 1940 1t was 70.800 B/D. EAST COAST shipments have averaged :.
25,100 B/D so far in the current month - up 7.900 B/D over the like period last
month - 1,800 B/D higher than the average for June 1940. It has been reported that
the tankers formerly used in the California/East Coast run will be taken for use in

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.98)
27. 1941 - Bulletin No. 1941-25
2
the Gulf/East Coast movement. If this proves true, California will lose a market
for about 4,000,000 bb1. of gasoline and 500,000 bb1. of miscellaneous products
annually. These figures are based on what the estimated 194l movement would have
been after having already lost the crude and fuel business developed back there in
1939 and which dvindled away in 1940. However, as in the case of declines in other
offshore business, a surging: domestie demand is expected to more than counteract
the anticipated stoppage of East Coast shipments. A further possihl offo t to the
curtailment of East Coast snipments may lie in the recent statement by the President
suggesting aid to Russia. It is possible that efforts vill be made to furnish the
Russians what they need and they probably will need gasoline. Last year California
shipped 1,130,000 bbl. of petroleum products,mostly gasoline, to Russia, and in 1941
up to the present the total stands at 503,000 bbl., also mainly gasoline. Most of
the shipments have been made in American tankers but nevertheless the Russians used
three of their own wiiich under present turn of circumstances could yet be used to
haul the cil. The Aneriean tankers  revioisly in this trade have been transferred
or nave  cn diverted to other runs after the Maritine Comiission denied charters

Reference Tokyo's despatch 5653, June 11,
1941.
This despatch contains considerable de-
tailed confidential information obtained by
officers of the Embassy at Tokyo from the
Commercial Secretary of the Netherlande
Legation at Tokyo.
It 1s of interest to note the close co-
operation between the British and Dutch
representatives at Tokyo and the attempts made
by the Japanese to induce the British to assist
in bringing about a successful conclusion of
the negotiations at Batavia.
The despatch is largely devoted to a
report of the negotiations regarding rubber.
The Japanese at one time sought 30,o00 tons
from the Netherlands Eest Indies, claiming that
Japan's annual requirements were 96,950 tons.
The Netherlands East Indies reply to the
Japanese offered only 15,000 tons of rubber per
year.
The Embassy found somewhat surprising, in
view of the importance of rubber to national
the fact that shipments of scrap rubber
United States to Japan increased
during

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.98)

during 1941. The Japanese in talking with the
Dutch apparently chose dellberately to give the
impression that shipments of scrap in were no
longer being received from the United States.
The reverse is true - shipments have continued
and have increased.
This despatch contains much material of
interest to anyone making a detailed study
of Netherlands East Indies - Japan nepotlations.

Mr. De Roos called at the Embassy on
June 4th and made the following statements concern-
ine te soatus at that time ofbe Jao nese-
Netherlands Indies trade negotiations, with parti-
cular reference to the supply of rubber to Japan.
On May 26, 1941, the Minister of Foreign
Affairs, Mr. Matsuoka invited the British Ambassador
to call upon him and on the same day Mr. Ohashi, the
Vice Minister, invited the Minister for the Netherlands
to visit him. According to Mr. De Roos, the
Japanese-Netherlands Indies trade negotiations were
discussed at both meetings. Mr. Matsuoka reportedly
requested Sir Robert Craigie to have Great Britain
use its good offices in making the negotiations a
success. Sir Robert passed this information on to
General Pabst.
Mr. Ohashi told General Pabst that Japan
wanted the negotiations to reach a successful con-
clusion and that Japan would hate to take steps that
otherwise might be necessary. General Pabst pointed
out that his country was determined to do everything
possible to keep rubber and other vital commodities
from getting into the hands of the enemy.
Sir Robert and General Pabst made Full
reports

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
3
reports on these meetings to their respective govern-
ments, 1ncludinf the Governor General at Batavia.
They were advised to have their respective commereial
secretaries in Tokyo confer with officials of the
Japanese Government in order to ascertain Japan's
actual requirements for rubber. Messrs. De Roos and
Macrae estimated ti.at a total of 55,000 long tons of
crude rubber per annum from all sources would be
ample to meet all Japanese demands. This estimate
was then cabled to London and to Batavia.
Note:-
No account was taken of the scrap and
reclaimed rubber imported annually from the
United States to the extent of around 23,000
tons or about 11,500 tons of rubber.
On May 3Oth Mr. De Roos called on Mr.

Office. Mr. Mizuno stated that the Japanese Govern-
ment appreciated the objection of the Netherlands
Government to letting Germany receive even one ton of
rubber from the Indies. Mr. De Roos then called his
attention to the press campaign concerning the dead-
looked state of the negotiations and pointed out
that all of these reports were one-sided and that the
contents of some of the articles showed that they
were based on material supplied by the Japanese Govern-
ment, which was contrary to the spirit of the negotia-
tions. Mr. De Roos intimated that some of the
artioles might even have been offioially inspired.

House of Representatives,

My dear Congressman:
I aclmowledge receipt of your letter of June 2nd in
answer to mine of May 12th, the answer to which was delayed
because of my absence Irom the city.
I have taken the subject of your letter up with my
client, and for your information, the name of the company
is the Loewenthal Co. This company is a corporation and no
actual residence is maintained except through the officers,
one of whom is a resident in your District, Mr. M. S.
Ruhman, and of course I also am a resident of your District.
The company maintains 1ts offices at 188 W. Randolph St.,
Chicago, Ill.
For your information, the State Department was consulted
about the action in withdrawing licenses to Japan, and
although a sympathetic ear was turned in our direction,
nevertheless a flat refusal to permit the exporting of
rubber by the company was finally made.
As previously advised you, the license agreements were
issued directly to the Japanese Consul in the United States,
which agreements were presented and displayed to the
Lowewenthal Co. Acting upon the issuance by our State
Department of such licenses and believing at all times that
if the same were revoked or cancelled, the company would
be notified in the proper time. The State Department had
knowledge that a tremendous amount of tires were being
purchased and shipped for exportation against these
licenses. Nevertheless, without notice to the company,
and after commitments were made to export this merchandise,
and after the same was purchased all over the country for
such purpose and after several thousand dollars had been
spent for freight, storage, handling, etc., the licenses
were revoked causing irreparable losses.
I believe

-2-
I believe that a clearer picture of the entire trans-
action can be given to you if you will but permit me to
call unon you in Was' in~ton at your convenience, which I
will be very happy to do, and for which upnointment I will
await your word.
Please accept my bent wi shes for the interest you have
displayed in this matter, and rest assured that i the
opportunity presents itself, I will gladly reciprocate.
With kindest personal regards, I remain

Dear lir. Smith:
Replyiny to your letter of May 12th, will you please
advise me the name of the client involved in the rubber
transaction, about which you wrote me. If your principal
is a resident of Chicago, please furnish me with his
address so that I might determine whether or hot he resides
in the Second Congressional District.
I am personally at a loss to understand how the
State Department would be guilty of any action that would
be of such an arbitrary nature as to impose loss on
citizens of this country. I, of course, appreciate the
importance of rubber in any form. It would seem to me,
if you have not already done so, that all of the facts
in the case ould be developed by taking the matter up
direct with the State Department. Frankly, I shall not
agree to introduce any private bill unless the parties
involved are residents of my district, and further that
I am satisfied that it is a case which would justify my
taking such action. I shall determine that after I have
heard further from you.

Hon. Raymond S. Meleouth,

Washington, . C.
My dear Congressman:
I am taking the liberty of again calling upon you for
your assistance in a matter with which I am entirely un-
familiar, having in mind the srlendid cooperation I
received from you heretofore and ho inr that you will find a
few moments to give me.
I represent a scrap rubber company which company has
been exporting scrap rubber to Japan on licenses secured
by the State Department to the Japanese Government.
Relying upon various licenses issued, my client arranged
to ship to various ports in the United States several
thousand tons of scrap rubber for shipment to be made on
certain boats. After the rubber was delivered to the docks
my client was advised that all permits had been withdrawn
by the State Department and consequently the rubber could
not be exported.
The loss incurred by my client is between lo,O00 and
$15,O00 and I believe that in view of the fact that my
client was lulled by the Government into a position where
these shipments were made some compensation should be
forthcoming.
It may therefore become necessary for me to call upon
you to introduce the proper Bill before your Honorable Body
wi th a view towards having the same referred to the Court
of Claims for an adjustment if such is the procedure.
If it is your desire that I come to Washington, to
discuss this matter more fully with you, please advise me
when it will be convenient for you to see me.
Please accept my best wishes and I trust that you are
enjoying your usual good health.
Very sincerely yours,
LOUIS A.SMITH

894.24
that the comments of the Consulate would be of interest, in
t
view of the shortage of fuel in Morocco and the dependence of

811.200
heads of the ore, whioh " is now finfing its way to the Amis
PS/DM
Powers,"on the question of the vital importance that the fuel

Since the above despatoh was written, conditions as regards
méning operations and the shipment of minerals abroad have ook-
siderably changed. According to the Chief of the Diplomatie
Cabinet of the French Residency Generat at Rabat ( Mr. du Gard-
ier ) in a recent interview, the Vichy Government has given de-
fimite orders that no cobalt, molybdenum or manganese shall be
aperted to any country whatsouver. Not only the Axis Powers
 also the United States are affeoted by this order.

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
It is respectfully pointed out that, even if this was not
true, it would be comparatively easy to check, on the part of
our Vice-Consuls, who under the economic accord will be watch-
ing to see that there are no re-exports of articles imported un-
der the agreement, and who in the perforjance of this phase of
their duties could see whether such ore shipments are going on from
various ports.
But the Consulate does not disbelieve the above official
statement. Furthermore, it is unlikely that any substantial
amounts of minerals have at any time been shipped to the Axis
Powers since the collapse of France and before the above order
from Vichy.

above despatch was ritten, but for one reason or another have

might find its way to the operation of lines from the mines to
the main railway lines and thence to the coast, the above Viohy
prohibition, if maintained, and, still more, the crying need of
the bulk of such fuel for the operation of agricultural machin-
ory for the coming Moroccan harvest will for some time to come
make any extensive movement of minerals in undesired directions
unlikely.

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
RECEIVED
DATE: July 23, 1941
British Government Poliey With Reference to Japan
SUBJECT:
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Neville Butler, Mr. Noel Hall, Mr. Acheson
94.24/1
1424
COPIES TO:
U - Mr. Welles
Mr. Butler and Mr. Hall called on me at their reques
Mr. Hall stated that the Embassy had received a telegram
London regarding the economic measures which they expected
put into effect against Japan and stated that the British
Government had recommended to the Dominions that a full
894.24
freezing of Japanese and Chinese assets sould be institut
The Government was still awaiting replies from the Domini
but Mr. Hall had no doubt that assent would be given. He
said that the British Government had taken up with the
Netherlands Government, the Belgian Congo, the Free French
authorities the matter of instituting similar action.
I asked whether, as administered by the British,
freezing control would carry with it control over exports
and imports, and he said that it would. I asked on what
basis the British would be prepared to permit exports and
imports. He replied that he had understood from my earlier
conversation with him that we were considering specific
licenses for exports to equal strategic materials which 1t
MAY1 3 1941
might be necessary for us to obtain from Japan, and that he
understood that the British Government would be prepared to
operate on the same basis. He also understood fromm that
it was our intention at the outset not to disturb the
existing export controls from the Philippines and that it
would

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
一 2 -
would be the British intention to adopt the same principle
so far as Malaya was coneerned.
In accord nee with instructions from the Acting Secre-
tary, I stated to Mr. Hall that the exact situation in
Indo China was still not clear to this Government, and that
the time table for putting restrictions inlo effect by this
Government and the order and extent of the restrictions would
depend upon the facts as they developed. I warned him that
my prior conversation with him was not to be interpreted as
meaning that this Government would put all the controls into
effect simultaneously or immediately, but that we would give
him ample notice before taking any action and would inform
him of the action proposed to be taken. He said that he
would appreciate this very much since this would give an
opportunity for the British Government to communicate with
the Dominions.
Mr. Butler asked me whether theconference between Mr
Welles and the British Ambassador had thrown any new light
on the situation. I said that Mr. Welles had informed me
that it had not; that Mr. Welles would make an appointment
to discuss the matter with Mr. Butler on Thursday, July 24.
Dean Acheson
A-A:DA:BE

MARR FURNITURE COMPANY
Will Marr, Proprietor
Ralls, Texas
July 12, 1941
Hon. George Mahon
Washington, D. C.
Fri end George:
I notice in yesterdays Star Telegram that the Japanese are loading
3 or 4 large transports with crude oil from the docks of Los Angeles,
California.
The amount in barrels is around 400,000. and I lieve the majority
of the people of this country think thi an outrage. To sell these
Japs oil from our wells, to shoot
boys with if we happen to
get into a war with Japan, and it en
that this has been very close
for the past few years.
I do not and am sure there afe other millions in these United states
of America that do not under tand is. And we see no reason why
this Government should allow an enemy country to carry oil or any-
thing else of this natare away from this country, that they might
use in the future to fght with.
We are helping China, Ruseia, England in war materials in every con-
ceivable way possible. Yet at the same time we are furnishing
another nation war materials to destroy and kill out the other na-
tions we are trying to help.
This is hard to understand. Why should we furnish Japan with any-
thing that will help destroy China or England? If I had my way about
it, I would tell Japan in a very nice way to go some where else and
buy their war materials as we could not afford to furnish them with
materials to shoot back at us with, maybe sometime in the near future,
or for that matter to use against our friends we are trying to help.
I would write the President if I thought he would ever see the letter,
but guess it would be thrown away, so thought would write you and get
your opinion as to why we are allowing this to happen. If Japan can

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.98)
get 400,000 barrèls of oil she can get 4,000,000 just as easy, or
anything else of war material she may need. I am "agin" it tooth
and nail and believe that every red blooded American in America or
any other nation will be "agin" it.
Our boys are training now for war perhaps with Japan, anyway with
her allies, Ger any and Italy. Perhaps this oil will find its way
to Germany or Italy. Who knows? Are we going to furnish those
countries with war materials through Japan to kill and maim our
own boys with? Why are we doing this?
very respectfully yours,
Will Marr

 heve your letter in whicl yo nquire  t th reesons hy the

the Chimese, eto., but as a matter of public polioy the President

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.95)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 12, 1941
Bombay's despatch no. 253, July 7, 1941,
"Japanese Purchases of Indian Cotton".
The sudden increase in Japanese purchases
of Indian cotton 1s expected to result in the
shipping of 240,000 bales from India to Japan
1n July and August. This will bring shipments
for the cotton year [September 1940 to August
1941] to 1,005,000 bales, an increase of
180,000 bales over the previous year. Since
Japanese imports will fill almost all avail-
able space in Japanese bottoms, little cotton
will go to Shanghai in the summer months, and
the Shanghai 1mports of Indian cotton are ex-
peoted to fall from the figure of 525,000 for
the previous year to 372,000 bales.
894.24/1497
40110
eoR
Mk. ACHESON

Departmenl of State
SUBJECT:
Japanese Purchases of Indian Cotton
1—1055
Ye 
THE HONORABLE
THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
WASHINGTON.
SIR:
894.24
I have the honor to refer to my telegram of July
3rd (1941) 12 noon, and to report that reliable sources
in Bombay estimate total shipments of Indian cotton to
Japan (including Korea and Manchuria), from September 1,
1940 to June 30, 1941 at 765,000 bales of 400 pounds each.
During July and August of 194l, an estimated total of
240,o0o bales will be shipped from India to Japan. The
DCA
total shipments, therefore, to Japan during the Indian

Dear Mr. Hamilton: ?. 194
yesterday afternoon, concerning supplies of 86 Octane Gasoline
which the Japanese are anxious that we make available to them in
Shanghei, we have received certein information from our West Coast
representative which we are taking the liberty of quoting and
which we think you will find of interest:-
"(Under date of July lst) An interesting story concerning
Japanese business revolves around Gasoline of the usual
lead. The Japanese are very much in the market for this
material but have been completely stymied by their
inability to obtain any action on the many license
applications which have been on file for the past four
months. It is reported that practically all Pacific Coast
suppliers have now almost exhausted licenses previously
obtained for the shipment of this material. Tidewater-
Associated, so it is said, has arranged with Mitsubishi to
cease shipping any gasoline as such and are utilizing the
P
balance of their licenses to blend into crude oil in order
to fill contracts previously made for such crude.
It will be recalled that when aviation gasoline was placed
on a license basis in July of l940, a great amount of this
工
material had been sold for shipment from the Gulf and very
shortly thereafter practically all suppliers were successful
in getting licenses for the entire amount of such gasoline
sold but on the lower specification of less than 87 ootane
AUG1-1941
instead or 87-plus. At that time Cities Service Oil Company
EILED
was supposed to have had on order about 500,000 drums, plus
89
two or three cargoes of this material. rairly large ship-
ments were made in drums but long before the licenses were
exhausted the embargo was deolared on steel containers,
effeotively stopping that business. Also- the Japanese, of
course, ceased sending any tankers to the Gulf which made
it impossible to perform on the contracts for bulk shipment.
As a result, Cities Service is said to have remaining
licenses for the equivalent of at least three cargoes of
this material but with no way of utilizing them and the
business was considered to have died.
A new twist was given, however, when no further licenses began
to be issued for shipment from the Pacifio Coast, whereupon

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.97)
Mr. Marwell M. Hamilton.
- 2 -
July 18, 1941.
"Gities Service, so the story goes, were successful in
obtaining permission from the State Department to change
the shipping point on their licenses from the Gulf to
the racific Coast. The licenses themselves, of course,
are not transferrable, so Cities Service arranged to
purchase either one or two cargoes of this material from
Richfie.d and one from S.0. of calif. and shipment has
been made and will be made from the racific coast in the
name of Cities Service and for the account of Mitsui."
(Under date of July 15th) It is now reliably reported that
Republic Petroleum Company of Texas City, Texas, have
licenses for about 250,oo0 barrels of the regular grade
Japanese Aviation Gascline which was sold to Nissho for
shipment fron the Gulf. The one year time limit on these
licenses expires on September 5, 1941, and Republic have
asked several Pacific Coast companies to quote them on this
amount of gasoline for shipment from the Pacific Coast.
Either Republio have obtained permission to change the

anticipate they will be able to do so.
So far as can be learned, no new licenses have as yet been
issued on Gasoline, and also the granting of licenses for
Grude Oil has become very slow."

Memorandum of Conversation
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
DATE: July 12, 1941
SUBJECT: Question whether the Stanidard-Vacuum Oil Company and other
interested oil companies should supply to the Japanese
Army at Shanghai from the Dut ch East Indies or other
sources outside the United States gasoline of 86 octane
neranrsrating in return for permission from the Japanese to
ship petroleum products to various points on the lower
Yangtze under the companies' own trade name.
Participants: Mr. A. G. May of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
26 Broadway, New York.
GOP ES TO
Mr. Hamilton
Assic'an: C crotary
FW 894.24/1513
of State
Mr. Adams
JUL 15 1941
RC
894.24
Te
MR. ACHESON
1-1408
Mr. May called at the Department as the result of an
appointment made at his request by long-distance telephone
from New York.
Mr. May handed to Mr. Hamilton the attached letter
dated Jume 10, 194l outlining the problem which Mr. May
had in mind and asking for the Department's views in
P
S/LDP
rogard to that problem.
Mr. May mid that for a good many months the
Standard-Vaouum Oil Company had refrained from supplying
to Japan proper or to the Japanese forces in China gasoline
that could be used for aviation purposes, either from the
United States
1513

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
United States or the Dutch East Indies. He said that
while n outri ht po usal tad seen made to the apanese
the Standard-Vacuwm il Comp any had raised the question
of scarcity of tankers and taken the position that so far
as the Dutch East Indies were concerned aviation gasoline
was, because of lim:ted  roduction facilities and prior
comnitments, simply not available. He said that all of the
oil companies had been under considerable pressure from the
Japanese to supply to Japanese forces aviation gasoline.
Mr. May said that his company had followed the policy
of scrupulously obeying the spirit as well as the letter
(of the export restricti ons imposed by the United States.
He said that, for example, if the regulations cf the
United States prohibited the exportation from the United
14
0
States to Japan or to occupied China of petroleum products
of specified grades the company's practice was to refuse to
supply petroleum products of such specified grades from
any source even though that source was not covered by the
restrictions.
Mr. liay said that there were some angles to the
problem not covered in his letter to Mr. Hamilton. One
of these was that it might seem inconsistent for the
company to supply gasoline of 86 octane rating to the
Japanese

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.96)
-3-
Japanese lovernnt in inn wn at the same time
refusingsurlya
The
pade to the
Japanese governaent in Ja an. Al , co . I ance wit the
Japanese request for gasoline of an 86 octane rating
from the Dutch East Indies  ight seem to be inconsistent
wi th the co pany' repeated statement t the apunese
Government that gasoline of such a high rating was not
available in the Dutch East Indies. These point s, Mr. May
indicated, were arguments against meeting the Japanese
desires. He indicated that the arguments in favor of
meeting the Japa ese desires were (1) that to do so would
be conducive toward restoration of normal trade for the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company in central China and (2) that
failure to meet the Japanese desires would be considered
by the Japanese as clear evidence of the company's policy
and would result in even greater restrictions upon and
interferences with the company's business than were now in
effect.
Mr. May said that he felt that it was quite possible
that his company would, whatever indication the Department
of State might give of its views in the matter, decide not
to accede to the Japanese request. He added that of course
this would not be the case if the Department of State
should

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
should express a clear des:re ti. t the apanese request
be met. mr. may asked whe t er he could be i ven some
indication of the attitude of the Department of State toward
the proposal under consideration.
In reply Mr. May was informed that, as he was of
cairse aware, the United states Government was conmitted
to assisting China in China's resistance to Japanese
attack and that consequently the Government of the United
States was not desirous of encouraging any action on the
part of an American company which would tend to assist the
Japanese attach upon China. r. May was further informed,
however, that the proposed action did not appear to be
contrary to any law or regulation of this Government and
that it was felt that the Government would not, under such
circumstances, wish to attempt to influence the decision
of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company in the matter. The company
knew its own particular problems better than anyone else and,
as it must succeed or fail in its enterprises upon the basis
of its own decisions, it was felt that the company should feel
free to make its decisions in the light of its own special
information and of the situation with which the company was
contronted.
Mr. May

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
Mr. May asked whe ther, if his company should decide
not te aeeude t tre dapumese request, mis cumpany could
expect ef ec: ve ass stace tr the ited States Govern-
ment in regard to difficult ies tiat might and probably would
grow out of his company's refusal to mect the Japanese wishes.
In reply Mr. l ay was informed that, as he was aware, the
dovernment ot the tnited states had on many cccasions  re-
tested against interference witi! the rights of the Standard-

Mr. May indicated that he had in mind action by the
American Government in addition to protests. Mr. May was
given to understand that it was altogether probable that
any retaliatory measures which the American Government
might take against the Japanese in China would, in view of
the large issues involved, probably have to rest on a
broader foundation than any specific single instance of
interference by the Japanese with the business of an
American firm. Mr. May indicated that he understood this.
Mr. May was informed that Mr. Hamilton and Mr. Adams
desired to bring his request to the attention of other
officers of the Department and would undertake to communicate
with him by telephone not later than Monday or Tuesday of
next week. Mr. May agreed that this would be satisfactory
and thanked Mr. Hamilton and Mr. Adams for receiving him
and

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
JUL 10 1941
New York Times
WHAT NEXT FOR JAPAN!
Japan's "new policy," being hastily
improvised after the shock of the Nazi
invasion of Russia, has not yet been
officially revealed. When it is. one
must hope that it will prove wiser than
the policy Japan has been pursing for
the last four years. For on net balance,
what Japan has mainly to show for its
wanton attack on China is its own
gradualimpaverishment and upward of
1,500,000 of its sons killed or wounded.
And the end is not in sight.
But the military Teaders of Japan,
instead of learning the enormous les-
son that the China incident had to
teach, dream of boety and power to be
seized in the Netherlands Indies and
now, perhaps, even in Russia. With
this in view they may believe they have
much to gain from the temporary al-
Jiance with Germany. A time when
England and Russia are being kept
fully busy by Hitler, and when America
is directing its main efforts to insure
his defeat, may seem to the Japanese
leadership the ideal time for conquest
of weak neighbors. Further, the Japa-
nese, for would-be conquerors of the
Netherlands Indies, are woefully weak
in the air, and their leaders may figure
that if Hitler should completely subdue
Russia he could fly directly to them
over Russia the planes that are so in-
dispensable to their success.
But the example of Russia should be
enough to show them how illusory such
a vision is. Hitler never keeps even an
accomplice a day longer than that ac-
complice is useful to him. He turned
savagely upon his erstwhile Russian
collaborator without warning and after
he had had nearly two years of moral
and material support from Russia. He
will do the same thing to Japan if she
elects to become his real partner. If
Japan invades Russia or tries to divert
the United States from aid to Britain,
she will help to make Hitler more pow-
erful, she will help to make him her
neighbor, and she will help to make
herself eventually subservient to him.
She will hold only as much of China or
of anything else as it suits Hitler to
have her hold. As allianee with Hitler
would be the beginning of Japan's un-
doing even by the standards that he
leaders have reeently set for her.

DATIJUI
Quicken Our Pace
As millions of workers in America return today
to their jobs after a holiday week-end, there is the
need for firm resolve from ocean to ocean and from
Canada to the Gulf of Mexico to bend every effort to-
ward increased production for national defense.
This is not the time to relax, as some people seem
to be doing, just because Adolf Hitler's armies are en-
gaged with Russia. This is, instead, the time to make
haste, to prepare, to rush every possible aid to Britain
in order that free people will make the most of their
opportunities in the protection of freedom against nazi
slavery.
Rumblings in the Far East call for a firm posi-
tion there too. It's useless to spend millions of de-
tense and to rush aid to Britain and China IF, at the
same time, we sit back and let the dictator nations
gain power step by step and gradually thresten the fu-
ture of freedom-loving people. Japan will understand
 firm policy just as easily as she will understand that
he'll get her way if we have no policy at all.
This is the time for firmness in purpose and for
rmination in work. America must not be a "wish
d" country-America must be a "we must and

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF CONTROLS
U1 i
PLOEIVED
MEMORANDUM
:104
August 28, 1941.
Mr. Adame of FE handed me thie morning the attached
letter dated August 4 and enclosures from the Deputy
Petroleum Coordinator. With apologies Mr. Adams stated
that Mr. Peck had been holding this letter while he under-
took a study to determine the accuracy of the statement
contained in the second paragraph concerning the juris-
diction of this Department over policies and regulations
governing the exportation of petroleum and petroleum
products.
I told Mr. Adams that 1t was my impression that
Mr. Davies' statement was correct but that I could not
cite him chapter and verse. Mr. Adams said that Mr. Peck
had apparently satisfied himself that the responsibility
rested with the Administrator of Export Control by virtue
of the Act of July 2, 1940.
Is there anything which I can tell Mr. Adams to set
his mind at rest?
Rud
Edward L. Reed
Attached:
Letter from Deputy
Petroleum Coordinator
dated August 4, 1941,
and enclosures.

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.97)
INTERIO
RECEI
JUL 25
OFFICE
THE SECR
7 St. Clair Avenue
INTERIOR DEHT
Old Greenwich, Conn.
July 24, 1941
JUL 2 5 1941
COTLON P
The Honorable Harold Ickes
Secretary of the Interior
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Secretary:
We are in thorough accord with your policy
of trying to conserve oil but we are at a
loss to know why so much emphasis is being
placed on this at the present time while we
are still supplying more than 60 of the oil
used by Jepan's war machine as reported in
the attached clipping from the New York
Herald Tribune of Thursday, July 24.
It is my privilege to come into contact with
a great many people and to date I have been
unable to find any one who approves of or
understands why we are still supplying Japan
with such large quantities of oil.
As Secretary of the Interior, doubtless you
are in a position to give an explanation.
I would appreciate hearing from you.
Very truly yours,
AMseoneho
A. M. Stonehouse

July 23, 1941.
The President,
The white house,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. President:
As a famous mun once a id: "You can't aupease a
tiger by stroking it."
Japan's imminent invasion of Frenoh Indo-China
proves, for the steenth time, that it 1s ridioulous for our
state Department to follow the policy of appeasement. wit-
ness the recent release of the French Oil tanker that reoently
stupped off at Dakar and unloaded a substantial quantity of
oil for the benefit of the Naz1 foroes stationed there.
We are fighting Fascism.
. Well, let's fight the
Fascists, -- wherever they may be, -- whether at Rome or in
Berlin, whether at Viohy or Tokio. For God's sake, let's
stop giving them weapons that may well eventually kill American
men, women and ohildren.
Japan has for too long a time been playing the same
"suoker game" on us that Italy played on France, -- up until the
very moment that Italy invaded France.
In the name of common
sense, let's stop supplying gangsters and kidnappers with bullets.
Yours very respectfully,
Widn Waffer
Isidor shaffer.
Copy to Honorable Harold L. Ickes.

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.98)
Third Street Methodist Church
931 East Third Street
WILLIAMSPORT. PA.
W. ARTHUR FAUS. MINISTER
July 4, 1.41
INTERIOR
ojoc.
RECEIVI
JUC-71941
7-1341
Hon. Harold Ickes
OFFICE OF
Seoretary of the Interior
THE SECRETA
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Ickes:
Recent threats or warnings from your office to the effect
that the eastern part of the United States may soon be required
to make drastic curtailments in the use of gas and oil are very
disturbing to many of us. We are willing and glad to make any
reasonable sacrifice that is necessary in the interest of national
defense, but we are not convinced of the necessity for any such
sacrifice at the present time, when our country is not at war and
does not want to go to war.In particular, it seems entirely
unjustified to make such drastic demands on the eastern part of
the United States as long as American gas and oil companies are
allowed to keep on selling these war products to Japan and to
Germany, as they are now doing. If our government is really in
earnest about its moral opposition to the ruthless barbarism of
Germany and Japan, she ought to put an immediate stop to all
selling of gas and oil to these countries before demanding that
any of her own citizens sacrifice these vital products.
As a Christian minister, I am particularly concerned about
the proposed plan for "gasless Sundays." Ir, after the hypocricy
of Amerioa selling oil to Japan and Germany has been abolished,
there is still a legitimate need for curtailing the consumption
of gas and oil in this country, I sincerely hope that such cur-
tailment will not prohibit the use of automobiles for carrying
people to and from church services. Not only in rural sections,
but also in the cities among people who live several blocks from
their church the prohibiting of the Sunday use of automobiles
would be a disastrous blow to the attendance at our churches.
It would be the height of folly, in the interest of conserving
gas and oil for war uses, to strike a serious blow at the insti-
tution which is maintaining the indispensible moral and spiritual
life of our nation.
Sincerely yours,
U. Arthun Fana

Dear r. Secretary:
OFFICE OF
THE SECRETA
I t: in you will find, in connection wih
your current campaign to cut down the use of gasoline in the
East, that people here will be glad to cooperate more
effectively if and when the ambiguous policy of the State
Department toward shipping oil to Japan is more happily
solved.
We are as eager as any other part that
supplies get to England, and that England win the war if she
can. We do not, on the othher hand, seo why our American oil
has to keep the Japanese machine going, when there are tank
cars available to ship oil to the East.
You are to be congratulated on your
prompt action, in response to a demand that a certain Jaranese
oil shipment from the East coast be halted so e time ago. My
guess is that you will find greater success in the situation
you face here if instead of blowing out about arresting people
for driving old jaloppies which leak gas, you begin stopping
shipments of gas to our enemies, no matter from which one of
our coasts.
In this connection, your influence would
be useful in helping along the current and porsistent campaign
to bring to light all the appeasing if not actually pro-Nazi
corners of the State Departnent. If you don't know about this,
keep up on your New Republic and Nation; they are doing a good
job in showing why our supposed "national" program for stopping
Hitler seems to be getting under way so slowly. All this
beind the facade of Mr. Hull's and the President's vigorous
speeces.
With best wishes,
Yours sincerely,
Dery Muin..I.
Hon. H. L. Iokes,
Washington, D. C.

LA JOLLA,CALIFORNIA
1941
July 25, 1941
The Honorable Harold L. Ickes,
Secretary of the Interior,
Washinrton,
Dear Sir:
Addressing you in your capacity of oil administrator
it is with great regret that we see oil tankers still
being loaded at Pacific Coast ports for Japan.
Californians have never been ab?e to understand the
appeasement of the Japs unless it has been a sop to
the oil companies who thus dispose of our irreplace-
able natural resources. Press reports state that
there has been no oil embargo so as to prevent the
Japs from moving into Southern Asia. Has this done
so? I hope this pussy-focting will now draw to a
close.
Very truly yours,
Caton M. MacKay
Eaton M. MacKay, M.D.

July 1, 1941
Honorable Harold L. Ickes,
Secretary of the Interior,
Washington, D. C.
Yy dear Mr. Ickes:
I have listered to you over the radio recently,
and heard your arraigrmert of Lirdberrh, and quite
agree with you, and I ar with you or o hor things;
bu: there is onep point at which I take issue: Ihe send-
ing of oil to Japan at a tire wher the conservation of
oil is recessary to the deferse of this courtry.
An embargo is put upon it along all the eastern sea-
board, but what under the sun do you allow it to be ex-
ported from the western seaboard? This is inconsistency!
It 1s flagrart hypocrisy to loan morey to China to carry
on its war of defense, and then furnish Japan with material
for making war on China.
This sort of double dealing certlinly will make His
Fajesty of the nether world shake his sides with laughter.
Some months ago at the questioning of the investigating
committee, a representative of the Standard Oil Co. was being
questioned as to the amount they exported to Japan. The matter
of putting ar embargo on its oil was brought up and the re-
presentative is renorted to have said: That if this was done
it would cripnle the Standard Oil Co.. Just think of that. I
ask is the U. S. government the obsequious tool of the Stand-
ard Oil? People are feeling ashamed of our goverrment in this
matter. Can you not, my dear sir, remedy it?
Sincerely yours,
farues Tooker Ford
JAMES TOOKER FORD.

Three of

On the night of June 6, one
of the most appalling dis-
asters of modern wartimes
occurred when 700 Chinese.
jammed into an air raid
shelter in Chungking, died
in the agony of suffocation
when the ventilating system
of the shelter broke down.

lished a foto of the wrecked
entrance to the shelter, but
today we publish these
three extraordinary photos

were removed. They show.
with overpowering elo-
quence, the cruel and uni-
versal recklessness with
which modern war pro-

=== Page 463 === (confidence 0.99)
Yes, Let's Blacklist the Axis
"U. S. MAY BLACKLIST PRO-AXIS
FIRMS."
That was last night's headline on one page
On another page was another headline:
"400,000 MORE BARRELS OF OIL
FOR JAPAN."
We're all for the blacklist idea. We think
firms which act as supply agents for the Axis
in the United States should be dealt with as
enemies of this country.
BUT—and this is a big BUT!—
The place to start is right in Washington,
in the department which is "urging" U. S.
oil companies to sell more oil to Japan—
At a time when Secretary Ickes is talking
about oil rationing for the Eastern seaboard,
and urging everybody to use less gasoline, and
prepare to use less oil for heating next winter.
Four hundred thousand more barrels of oil
for Japan means 400,000 more barrels of oil
for the Axis , Nothing less.
Japanlil a'partner in the Axis-the Far
East branch—offcially, by treaty, by policy.
and in the true Axis spirit of aggression.
But while we send cheers to China, Japan
usms our oil to bomb the Chinese.
Yes, let's have the blacklist.
And let it be started with an embargo on
oll to Japan.
NOW.

Dear Mr. Editof.-5.2-4/-
I want to conmend your edit-
orial in Satuptay's paper rela-
tive to the continued sale of oil
and gasoline to the Japanese. It
is ridiculous, to say the least,
for Secretary Ickes to suggest
the rationing of gasoline in this
country as long as we continue
to sell gas, or oil, or scrap iron
to a country which has allicd it
self with Germany and continues
to bomb our friends the Chinese.
May I suggest that it is such
inconsistent and contradictory
attitudes as this which under
mines confidence in the admin-
istration. Some of us begin to
believe with Senator Wheeler that
the President has aligned him-
seif with the same selfish pluto-
cratic element which led us into
the first World War, the "war
to end war." and "to save the
world for democracy."
Is it any wonder that, accord
ing to the Gallup poll. 79 per
cent of the American people are
opposed to going into the war.
Let us feed the five small dem
ocracies, let us held the Chinese
by ceasing to supply her enemy
with materials which may be
used for war. let its stiengthen
democracy within our own gates
and let us be ready to use our
nfluence for peace. Just now a
'Churchman's Campaign for
Peace Through Mediation," is
preading like wild fire across
he country. The movement is
ed by such prominent church
aders as the following: E. Stan-
ey Jones, George A. Buttrick,
Larry Emerson Fosdick, Albert
V. Palmer, C. C. Morrison, Albert
dward Day, E. M. Poteat, Bish-
p Holt, Albert Buckner Coe,
tc., etc., etc.

=== Page 470 === (confidence 0.99)
The Gasoline Situation
Seems Inconsistent to Us
and will continue to support, the policy of the
present administration in its effort to maintain the
perpetuation of American ideals and institutions
against the aggression or threat of aggression of
rampant dictators. Because of this position we be-
lieve we are privileged to offer constructive criticism
in those things which may appear to many Ameri-
cans to be unfair to them in respect to the sacrifice
the government expects each citizen to make.
We have one case i. point—substantiated by
newspaper reports of the past week. Secretary Iekes
suggested in a press conference that rationing of
gasoline may become a reality very shortly in the
east and other points in the country. The difficulty
of providing both for British export and home con-
sumption in the eastern area, according to the see-
retary. may even result in "gas-less Sundays" for
motorists.
During the past week. within view of Vallejo's
hill, two huge Japanese tankers have been loading
oil in Suisun Bay, destined for the Far East and, pre-
sumably. for consumption by the Nipponese fleet and
the Nipponese aireraft bombing Chungking. Similar
tankers have been weekly callers at oil wharves on the
waters of this bay.
Of course, one may argue that the real issue back
East is the lack of transportation facilities for oil
It may also be argued that regardless of any nationa
emergeney we shouldn't permit our foreign commerce
to suffer.
While these two reasons would seem to dispos
of the question, it is hardly a digestible picture to th
loyal American who is already informed he will pay
and pay until it hturts, for caur aid to Britain in com
mon defense against the axis powers of Europe, an
then expect him to lock up the motor car on Sunday
while Aimerican oil still drives the battleships and
planes of an axis power of Asia.
May we suggest to our leaders that Americar
oil be restricted to our allies and to ourselves. Th
Japanese have as firmly lined up with Hitler, Stali
and Mussolini as we have with Britain and the othe
democratic nations of the world. Why shouldn't w
then, treat the Japanese as we treat their comrade i
Europe? That's going to be a very real question i
the mind of any American motorist when asked t
ration his gasoline purchase and yet sees foreig
tankers sailing out through the Golden Gate, lad
with oil for Japanese warships and warplanes.

6/3/41.
How About Some Priorities Here?
Americanism: Talking about instituting
gasless Sundays for America's motoring
public and at the same time continuing to
ship gasoline to Japan.
The diplomat, oil czar or brain truster who
can justify this bit of insanity will have to do
some fast talking. Of course, no one has ever
explained the ridiculous situation that has pre-
vailed for the last few years, but it has con-
tinued nevertheiess. While mouthing words of
encouragement to China and even lending her
WILL YOU
money to carry on her defense against ruthless
SELLMEA
MAYBE
Jap aggression. we have sold petroleum, ma-

GALLON,

GASOLINE

SUPPLY

few months ago, we halted the flow of those
materials which had been reaching their ulti-
mate destination in China in the form of bombs
and shells.
But still we have continued to ship pe-
troleum, restricting only the export of certain
NOGAS
high-test gasolines. In March, Japan hauled

50 per cent increase over similar shipments in
March, 1940! Does anyone think this is just
being used to propel pleasure cars or motor-
driven rickshaws?
And now Harold Ickes, the government's
new czar of the petroleum industry, is talking
out adopting the World war practice of for-
Iding civilians to drive their cars on Sun-
y. Gasoline must be conserved, he says.
The American public will cheerfully co-
operate in any necessary plan for conserving
our resources in order to strengthen this coun-
try and its friends. Sacrifices will be made,
if necessary.
But Americans would be justly indignant
JAPAN

gallons into Japanese tankers.
Regardless of what the appeasement clique
in the state department may say, Honest Har-
old's first act as oil administrator ought to be to
shut off the flow to Japan.

Flemington, N. J.—(P)—The
Standard Oil Co. (New Jersey)
outlined to stockholders Tues-

Japan "negotiated with the full
