Ish and Netherlands East Indles
government."

that the question of oil shipments
to Axis powers had been raised
and the concern wished to ex.
plain its program.

interest in Standard Vacuum
Oll company, with far eastern
headquarters in The Nether.
lands East Indies.

"About six months ago this
affiliate joined with a Dutch-
British oil company, a major
producer of oil in The Nether
lands East Indies, in special ar-
rangement, over a limited period.
to sell Japan additional supplies
of oll and ordinary oll products.

gasoline, for example, was ex.
cluded.

the American, British, and
Netherlands East Indies govern-
ments.

with another country, Stand
ard Oll company (New Jerse)
must also maintain normal
lations.

initiative undertook to establish
embargoes on shipments of ofl
from one obuntry to another it
might find itself running direct.
ly counter to the polley and in-
terests of its own government.
Obviously it cannot permit itself
to be frawn into such a posi-
tlan."

REASONABLE EXEMPTION
BUT YOU
...WHO'S GONNA

MANAGER?
To the Editor:
Our "democratic" State Depart
ment, which works in a mysteri
ous way its wonders to perform.
has decided to send Hitler's little
helpers at Vichy two cargoes of
food a month. By some strange
alchemy this is supposed to win
the Vichy government over to
democracy. Instead of leading
them to think. as they do, that
we are blankety blank fools.
No wonder Hitler snickers at
our State Department for a bunch
of saps. Even the man in the
street knows you can't strengthen
democracy by catering to Fascist
and Nazi leaders. If certain in-
dividuals in the State Department
haven't been able to get that
through their heads in 10 years, it
is high time to remove them and
to substitute men with courage
and common sense.
J. K. R.

MEMBER OF THE ASSOCIATED PRESS
LOS ANGELES (Loce Ahng hail ais)
A THOUGHT FROM THE BIBLE
My soul waiteth for the Lord more than
they that watch for the morning: I say,
more than they that watch for the morn
ing.—Psalm xix, 12.
Is It Necessary?
Gasless Sundays, daylight saving, dark
ened places of night entertainment are all
right with the patriotic citizens of this
country if actual need for such recourse
should appear. Thinking people, however,
will want more than the word of Secretary
Ickes that the need exists. They will at
least want to know that this particular
Cabinet member is speaking with Presiden-
tial authority and not merely for the en-
joyment of hearing himself talk, as he has
done so often in the past.
Gas rationing would seem to be scme-
thing that might be postponed until the
present shipments of this fuel to Japan and
Russia have been stopped; there is cer-
tainly no justification for defense curtail-
ment of domestic supplies while oll prod-
ucts are going to doubtful nations and in-
directly helping the very powers suspected
of aggressive designs against our hemi-
sphere. With regard to the Ickes argument
that tanker transportation facilities may
be lacking to take oll to the Atlantic sea-
board, it need only be pointed out that the
railroads have shown no sign of bogging
down.
Daylight saving is old stuff in thè East,
has ere this been resorted to in that part
of the country as an economic measure.
It has yet to be demonstrated that it would

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
Sunday driving may be the first casu-
alty in the United States under what
President Roosevelt has proclaimed as
an unlimited national emergency. If
some relinquishment of this privilege
is necessary to the purposes of defense,
surely the minor sacrifice will be borne
with good grace.
It seems hardly likely, however, that
the situation calls at once for all pri-
vate cars to be ruled off the road on
the Sabbath as some speculation has it.
Church attendance on Sundays has its
part in conserving national morale, as
does indeed a certain amount of recrea-
tional use of motorcars. Yet probably
a considerable mileage in needless joy-
riding and in long week-end trips
would be inhibited by a regulation that
filling stations should be closed from
Saturday noon until Monday morning.
This might apply only in the eastern
United States, where transportation is a
problem.
In the western United States chief
interest in the order will be in its
application to the oil producing indus-
try. In this respect there will be a great
deal of apprehension due to the selec-
tion of Secretary Harold L. Ickes as co-
ordinator or "czar" of the industry,
though the choice is a very natural one
in view of the close contact of the De-
partment of Interior with the conserva-
tion of all natural resources including
petroleum.
Mr. Ickes, though he has criticized
severely the wastefulness of old com-
petitive methods in producing petro-
leum, is understood not to be in sym-
pathy with the most extreme tenden-
cies in the Cole Committee in Wash-
ington toward socialization as a remedy.
It is to be hoped that he will proceed
with a great deal of care for preserving
private initiative in the industry and
with an appreciation for what oil men
have accomplished through voluntary
co-operation and State laws.
Finally, however, the executive order
for oil conservation raises one impera-
tive question which is not so much for
the Department of Interior as for the
State Department.
This question is:
If "gasless" Sundays and stricter pro-
duction controls are necessary to a
sure that "the supply of petroleum and
its products will be accommodated to
the needs of the Nation and the ne-
tional defense program," then why in
the name of reason does the Unite
States permit gasoline and lubricating
and fuel oil to be shipped-as it ha
been from West Coast ports for months
at the rate of a tanker every twod
to an avowed ally of the Axis, Jaj
Production is going up, prices are
going up, appropriations are going up.
taxes are going up. Wonderful thing.
The Christiars Saumer
stov, J31941

the step be the first toward making the oll industry

business. Naturally bil men do not care to comment
工h
pubhcly on their new boss but the trade paper,
"The Oll Weekly" In an editorial by Ray L. Dudley,
publisher. says today:
"If there ever was an industry which was con-
ducting its own affairs in a manner tó provide
ample supplies to the civilian and armed forces of
the nation, that industry is the oll industry.
'If there ever was a man-lill-fitted by tempera-
0
ment, by training. and by natural instinct to manage
 great industry, that man is Harold Ickes.
"There ls no oll shortage and will be nane unless
the inefficient handling of oil matters by Icke
brings it about. The President's action is simply
a frultion of a desire which has been in his mind
and that of Ickes for several years. That he dares
to do this is not only an evidence that practical
thinking is absent from some of the administration's
actions, but it is also evidence of the development
of a daring which, in the minds of many, will not
stop with one industry, but which aims at not only
the temporary control but the permanent control
h
of the nation's industries by a group surrounding
and including the President.
"In his radio speech, the President referred to
the fear which many people have that in this
struggle in which this nation is embarked, democratic
government will be lost. He did not say, and pos-
sibly did not realize, that the fear was a fear
of what he and his close advisers might do. That
such a fear has been warranted is evidenced by
the word which came last Saturday from Hyde
Park.
"What will the industry do? The oll industry
will do its very best. The American oll industry
can do nothing less than its very best, even though
that best will be hampered by the antagonistic
management and probable vindictive tampering of
 man who has no qualities nor specific traits
of ability to handle the job.
"But the oil industry. as an American industry.
operated by American citizens, is not yet comp lled
to give up the right of free speech not the right to
spire to a continuance of democratic principles
which entitles those engaged in it to own property
in their own right...
"We believe that the elements within America
wish to contribute to unity-but the President cer-
tainly is going about achieving it in a manner
calculated to. damage it, and to give rise to the
gravest fears of his ultimate aims."
Perhaps the most pointed criticism about Secre-
tary Ickes which one hears among oll men is that he
did not stick by them and testify in their behalf in
the Madison, Wisconsin, oll suit. The contention
has been that the staff of the Interior Department
knew of the co-operative arrangements made to keep
"distress" oi) off the market and that no anti-
trust suit should have been fled. Other oil men
say that Mr. Ickes never gave his approval to what
his aides did in encouraging the oil companies to
co-operate with respect to "distress" gasoline. In
any event, his record would have been less vulner-
abie, oil men say, if he, at least, had given to the
jury his version of what happened so that men
with no criminal intent might not have been con-
Victed on Ue technical grounds offered by the De-
partment of Justice.
However that may be. the oll industry will
watch vigllantly its second experience with the
Secretary of the Interior for the problem is not only
how to keep peace in the oil business but how to
make sure of an adequate supply of oil for all
emergency purposes.

=== Page 487 === (confidence 0.99)
Why the Tanker Diversion?
Wall Street Journal
Among the matters which Oil Coordinator
Ickes will find demanding his immediate atten-
tion are the questions why 50 American tankers
were diverted to British government service and
whether they should remain so diverted. On
these two questions hangs the whole task of co-
ordinating oil supply and its transportation with
the defense-aid program.
A month ago the Maritime commission asked
American companies owning oil tankers to turn
over 25 such to be assigned to Great Britain,
later increasing the number to 50 vessels. It
was explained that the transfer would enable
the British to conserve and make better use of
their oil transport facilities by confining their
tankers in the Atlantic service to the shorter
run between Canadian and United Kingdom
ports. Notwithstanding the fact that some 95
per cent of the Gulf coast oil consumed in our
eastern seaboard territory reaches it by water.
the American oil companies complied with the
request, though with grave misgivings. The de-
mand-supply situation in the industrianzed east
beyond reach of existing pipe lines was already
in close balance. yet 50 of the 260 tankers serv-
ing that territory were to be withdrawn.
This newspaper does not undertake to say
whether or not the Maritime commisson's diver-
sion request was wise or necessary. It desires
at present only to cail attention to the fact that
the petroleum industry casts doubts upon it
which cannot be lightly dismissed, which in all
fairness must be dispelled before the industry
can be called upon to construct either a new
fleet of tankers or some thousands of miles of
pipe lines to avert a threatening shortage of oil in
the eastern seaboard region. Great Britain's
real need of the tankers and the propriety of the
diversion should, moreover, be clearly establish-
ed before this eastern industrial region is asked
to go on short rations of fuel and motive power.
For neither new tankers nor pipe lines can be
completed in time to offset the consequences of
turning over 50 American tankers to Britain's
shuttlc service between Caribbean oil fields and
Halifax.
It seems to us that the oil industry rightly
takes an interest in the llowing figures. assum-
ing their correctness. in 1939, before the war
began, Britain tankers numbered 498, with ag-
gregate capacity of 3,264,000 tons. Tankers the
British acquired from Norway, Belgium, Holland,
and France numbered approximately 400, of 2,-
800,000 tons capacity. About 100 of these have
been sunk, leaving a balance of 798 ships, with-
out reckoning a small number of new tankers
launched. Normal oil requirements of the
United Kingdom, about 90,000,000 barrels annual-
ly, could ordinarily be supplied with 100 tankers.
Since civilian consumption has been cut to the
irreducible minimum it is calculated that its war-
time requirements do not exceed 100,000,000 bar-
rels, representing the usual service of about 125
tankers, or, allowing for slower movement in
convoys, perhaps 250 vessels under present con-
ditions. There remains, apparently, an excess
of something like 500 tankers under British con-
trol, exclusive of the 56 American ships lately
diverted to their use.
What, it is reasohable to ask, are these 500
British tankers doing? Are they by any chance
employed in serving the British export trade on
a business-as-usual basis, while American oil
companies have been asked to surrender ships
serving American consumers who must satisfy
their needs through the far most costly trans-
portation of pipe lines and railroad tank cars and
do with a greatly diminished supply while 'ad-
ditional carriers are constructed?
It may prove, when all the facts are disclosed,
that the diversion of American tankers is justifled.
No attempt has been made here to enter into
several technical questions concerning the ef-
ficiency of the Caribbean-Halifax shuttle as com-
pared with the normal movement from Gulf coast
to North Atlantic ports, with regard to which our
own oil men have some decided opinions. The
one important issue involved is whether British
oll interests have used or endeavored to use the
lease-lend law to further their commercial inter-
ests at the expense of American oil companies and
the consumers the latter serve.
It should not take the Maritime commission,
long to answer the questions here outlined. Cer-
tainly they should be answered—and publicly an-
sweredbefore radical changes are made at great
expense in our system of petroleum utilization.

GIVE THE PEOPLE THE FACTS
The overwhelming majority of the Ameri-
can people are willing to make sacrifices to
defend our country.
But they can't be expected to face sacrifices
cheerfully if they are not given a reasonable
explanation for them. They can't follow
orders efficiently unless they are given rea-
sonable commands.
Right now the people are being made more
and more confused over the threatened scar-
city in petroleum.
A committee of the American Petroleum
Institute has suggested to Secretary Ickes,
Petroleum Co-ordinator for National Defense.
reduction in automobile driving through "gas-
less Sundays" and a reduction of five degrees
of oil heat in homes.
The people, we believe. will be willing to
stop driving their cars on Sunday and to have
less heat in their homes if uccessary.
But on the same day this report was re-
leased in Washington. the president of the
Standard Oil Company of New Jersey, W. S.
Farish. reported to the annual meeting of
stockholders in Flemington. N. J.. that sub-
sidiary companics have made an agreement
to sell additional supplies of oil to Japan. The
agreement, he said. was negotiated with the
full knowledge of the American. British and
Netherlands East Indies Governments.
We don't doubt that the three Governments
did approve.
But we do doubt very much whether the
American people will shiver in their homes
with any great willingness unless they are
told why they can't have oil, and Japan can.
Here's another point of confusion:
The Petroleum Institute indicated a short-
age of both fuel oil and gasoline.
Yet the president of Standard Oil of New
Jersey told his stockholders that the shortage
would come first in gasoline, not in fuel oil,
and that his company is prepared to "look
after" present fuel-oil customers, although it
is not advising additional installations.
Still another point the American people
can't understand:
By selling oil to Japan we are not only help-
ing Japan fight China—a country we have
pledged to help under the lease-lend bill—but
we are helping Germany fight Britain, a
country whose defense we have made one
with our own.
A dispatch from Tokio on Tuesday—the
same day the Petroleum Institute made its
report—told of increased shipments of essen-
tial commodities from Japan through Russia
to Germany. The total reaching Germany Is
now 1500 tons.a day.
And among the supplies being shipped Ger-
magyis.ail.
Here's another point that an American
family, asked to give up its Sunday driving,
can't understand:

ears out
of service because the fuel they used was needed
for detense. The restrictions were soon lifted,
however, and many of these cars are now back

than a  car ago. The reason was that the with-

travel long, roundabout, time-consuming routes
from home to office or factory and vice versa, if
indeed. they could get there at all.

for every tour petsons. In England the ratio is
one to about 20. In a country of greater distances
and with approximately five times as many per-
sons using automobiles in their every-day ac-
tivity, the probable impact of a stringent re-
striction of gasoline on the American economy
is one that anyone with a cmle of minutes and
a sharp lead pencil should e little difficulty
in figuring out.

hop

Think this over:
In March of this year, latest month for
which figures are available, we sent Japan
50 percent more oil than we sent her in
March of 1940.
The total was 1,553,000 barrels.
At that rate, we are sending Japan more
oil than the United States used for fuel in
1939, according to the figures of the Federal
Power Commission.
Twelve times 1,553,000 barrels is 18,636,000
barrels.
Total U. S. oil consumption for 1939, our
peak year, was 17,425,000 barrels.
Japan cannot possibly be using all that oil.
What is she doing with it?
She is storing it, against the day she will
be in a position to make war upon us.
It is the story of France, all over again.
France sold war materials to Germany up
to the very day war began. Certain French
business firms then kept on selling Germany
war materials, through Spain.
To prevent those unpleasant truths from
reaching the French people, a rigid censor-
ship on such exports was proclaimed—
Exactly the way our own Department of
Commerce has now proclaimed a censorship
on all exports from the United States, which
covers up completely the oil exports to Japan

feel that any policy of maintaining
these shipments while we go without
could be justified there are doubtless

off the Japanese would insist that we
also stop shipment to China, and this
we should be loath to do. Secondly,
if Japan lost our supplies she might
be driven to attack the Dutch East
Indies from which we obtain a num-
ber of other valuable products.

policy is to keep Japan out of the

Italy and Germany in the event we
ettack them. But if they attack us—
and the sinking óf a ship such as,

be called an act of aggression—

save face.

the heading of "It's about time.
In addition to the closing of the German
consulates there is the quick move of
Secretary Harold L. Ickes as petroleum
co-ordinator to prevent the loading
of 252,000 gallons of lubricating oil on
a Japanese tanker at Philadelphia.
Scarcely a more incongruous trade
movement could have been imagined
than the permitting of this oil to be
sent to Japan from the eastern United
States seaboard while at the same time
this very area is being told that it faces
fationing of petroleum products, pos-
sibly gasolineless Sundays and cooler
houses next winter. because of a
scarcity of tankships to bring supplies
from the Gulf of Mexico, the West
Coast, and Venezuela.
The immediate question it raises is:
Will the Government act quickly
enough and positively enough to stop
other possible shipments of this sort
from Atlantic ports? The voluntary
co-operation of the oil companies in
the Philadelphia case seems to indi-
cate that no obstruction will be en-
countered from them.
The next question is how long and
in what volume the State Department
will continue to let gasoline, lubricating
oil, and fuel oil be shipped from the
American Pacific Coast to Japan to be
used against American purposes and in-
terests in the Orient
To be sure, there appears to be an
adequate supply of petroleum products
in the United States if distribution can
be arranged. But the Nation has seen
some other commodities which a few
years ago seemed plentiful become
scarce, and is grateful even for such
limited stockpiles as were accumulated.
Why not some stockpiling of oil re-
serves?

and gasoline accumulated at refineries
nd in storage, the means would be
for transporting them to the

GASOLINE SHORTAGE JUST
ANOTHER FOOLISH BUGABOO
RIOR
To the Editor of The Spokesman-
CFIS
Review: Yesterday I had an ex-
perience. A man who should have
known better told me that it looked
as though we would be out of gaso-
line by July 1. Another fellow.
who should also have known bet-
ter, spoke of purchasing a bicycle.
What for? Gasoline shortage com-
ing. Authority? They heard it
some place.
What is this, anyway? Some
more of the same brand of govern-
ment propaganda which got every
one all hot and bothered about a
shortage of manganese in the
United States? From personal ex-
perience and as a result of an in-
vestigation extending over seven
months, I found that there was
enough manganese available in the
United States to run the country
at three times this abnormal need
per year for a period of 200 years.
Let's take a look at the facts.
In all. principal oil fields in the
United States, all production is pro-
rated. To open up these fields and
allow them to produce, it would be
possible to fiood the gasoline mar-
ket in five days. What's behind
this new scare?
Ls it a build-up to raise the price
of gasoline? Is it a well-consid-
ered plan by the administration to
put Harold L. Ickes in control of
the oll industry?
If there is such an imminent
shertage of gasoline, why do we
continue to ship gasoline to Tokyo?
At present we have a gas "shert-
age!" From the looks of the pat-
tern, we will continue to get other

Gasless Sundays
It may be necessary, of course, for the United
States to put a ban upon Sunday motoring in order
to conserve gasoline. If it becomes necessary, the
'American people will submit with good grace and
good humor. It is most unfortunate, however, that
the first intimation that such a step may be neces-
mc
sary should come from Harold Ickes, Secretary of

lmg down on saybody, or anything.

An absolutely gasless Sunday now would interfere
greatly with the recreation of many millions of peo-
ple. Millions who work hard all week spend the week
end at their little summer camp or cottage. Or, if

rant, or take a picnic lunch along with them. That
sort of driving has come to be looked upon by doc-
tors, psychiatrists and plain average persons as good
and necessary. Nowadays, when many are working
long heurs on defense projects, a week end or a Sun-
day at the shore, or a long Sunday drive, is really
re-creation in that it creates new energy, rests tired
nèrves, and prepares them for the grind of the com-
ing week at lathe, workbench or desk.

Gasless Sunday
The problem of confining the Sunday use of
pleasure cars to "errands of necessity," as a
means of conserving gasoline, is far more com-
plex today than it was during the last war.
Through the voluntary co-operation of motor-
ists in World War days, states east of the Mis-
sissippi were able to contribute savings in
petroleum and petroleum transportation that
definitely eased threatened shortages.
However, in those days there were 1,500,000
cars on the road. Today there are more than.
30,000,000. In those days our highway system
was not the fine and articulated network of
communication that it is today. In those days,
a hundred-mile journey was a feat; today it is
a trifie. Then the automobile was a luxury;
today it is a necessity.
Secretary Ickes points out that there is
plenty of gasoline and oil, but that the sug-
gestion for gasless Sundays is brought about
by a lack of transportation facilities to the
Eastern States from the great oil fields of the
Southwest. Only the East would suffer, as 'a
consequence.
Because of the complete change in pleasure
transportation in the last twenty years, or so,
day. It is the one day of the wear
family plans to get away from home. Te shss
people of their one assured day of
and recreation.
Tackling the prok

efnitely eased threatened shortages.
However, in those days there were 1,500,000
cars on the road. Today there are more than.
30,000,000. In those days our highway system
was not the fine and articulated network of
communication that it is today. In those days,
a hundred-mile journey was a feat; today it is
a trifle. Then the automobile was a luxury;
today it is a necessity.
Secretary Ickes points out that there is
plenty of gasoline and oil, but that the sug-
gestion for gasless Sundays is brought about
by a lack of transportation facilities to the
Eastern States from the great oil fields of the
Southwest. Only the East would suffer, as a
consequence.
Because o the complete change in pleasure
transportation in the last twenty years, or so,
the imposition of Sunday restrictions is sp
cially severe. Sunday is the great motoring
day. It is the one day of the week when the
family plans to get away from home. To shut
off Sunday travel would deprive millions of
people of their one assured day of relaxation
and recreation.
Tackling the problem of gasoline conserva-
tion from the old angle of Sunday restrictions
is an anachronism. Since it is probable that
motor fuel savings will be brought about
through the voluntary action of motorists, co-
operation should be sought through some other
plan than the sacrifce of the famiiy holiday.
Perhaps some such plan as neighborhood auto-
mobile pools during the week would work less
mconvenience. Instead of five neighbors on a
street driving five cars to work, they might get
together and each use his car for a week in
turn, with the other four riding as passengers.
That is merely one suggestion. It is not
our business to tell the oil conservators how
to work out their plans, but it dos 
gasless Sunday could be devised to achieye the
desired remlt without eliminating the day
for recreation that many people have.

for meditation and complete rest that will be

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF THE AMERICAN REPUBLICS
MEMORANDUM
August 6, 1941
Mr. Walmeley:
Mr. Collado:
I refer to the attached telegram
No. 1026, August 6, 11 p.m. from R1o.
I think a reply should be urgently drafted
to the effect that the prospective shortage
of all petroleum products makes 1t highly
undesirable for any of these products to
be distributed in such a manner that they
will not further the objectives of inter-
American commercial relations or inter-
American defense. This 1s of course
related to the general subject of the
bunkering of Japanese vessels which
I understand you have been handling.
Phiiip W. Bonsal

Your 1026, August 6, 1941, 11:00 p.m.
894.24/1518
The following course of action is suggested pending
a definite statement of policy by this Government on the
bunkering of Japanese vessels in Latin American'ports:
Request all American oil companies operating in Brazil
to limit the sale of petroleum products to Japanese ves-
sels to the companies' estimates of necessary Ahipboard
needs, if not already the case, have the companies re
quest payment for these products in dollars; the Brazilian
Government may be informed, if this appears desirable,
that overstocking/by Japanese vessels/of petroleum products
in Latin American ports is not regarded favorably by
this Government, and should this continue it may be neces-
mary to take this factor into consideration An the alle-
eatiem of tanker tonnage under the proposed plen of thie
It is hoped that within a few daye

TOBK, PEBBLE BEACH, CALIF., JULY SI, 1941
THE PRESIDENT:
 UNHESITATINGLY RECOMMEND AN IMMEDIATE COMPLETE AND EFFECTIVE
EMBARGO AGAINST ANY FURTHER SHIPMENTS OF OIL OR PI TROLEUM
PRODUCTS AND OTHER WAR MATERIALS TO JAPAN. THE SENTIMENT OF THE
PEOPLE OF CALIFORNIA HAS FOR A LONG TIME BEEN AGAINST OUR SUPPLY-
894.24/1152
ING THESE WAR MATERIALS FOR JAPANESE AGGRESSIONS AND IN AID OF THE
PLANS OF THE AXIS POWERS DOING SO DOES NOT SEEM TO HAVE PREVENTED
AGGRESSIVE STEPS BY JAPAN IN INDO-CHINA AND TOWARD THE DUTCH EAST
INDIES. WE IN CALIFORNIA WOULD PREFER TO SEE THE TATUTAMARU WHICH
HAS NOW ARRIVED IN SAN FRANCISCO HARBOR WITH THOUSANDS OF BALES OF
SILK FOR AMERICAN CONSUMPTION IN ANTICIPATION OF RETURNING WITH OIL
SUPPLIES FOR THE JAPANESE WAR MACHINE TAKE ITS SILK BACK TO JAPAN
AND IN ANY CASE THAT IT BE NOT PERMITTED TO RETURN WITH OIL OR GAS-
OLINE OR ANY OTHER WAR MATERIALS. WE HOPE THE LICENSING SYSTEM WILL
NOT MEAN ANY FURTHER TOLERANCE OF JAPAN'S PURCHASES OF MATERIALS OF
WAR I THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA. WITH KIND REGARDS.
CULBERT L. OLSON, GOVERNOR OF CALYFORNIA.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
The Directors of the Passaic Taxpayers and Renters
Association have instructed me to write to you as
President of our Association making the suggestion that
no oil rationing be done in the East until sales and ship-
ments of oil and gasoline now being made to Japan have
been discontinued. The rationing of oil and gasoline
in and around our area will adversely affect the taxpayers
there; hence, this request.
Yours very truly,

5
"Inquiry at the Central Bureau of Registered
3
Addresses revealed that no cable address under the name

=== Page 552 === (confidence 0.98)

produced the application in question, which bore rubber stamps

cent higher than here, and that it was his contention that the
higher prices in Colombia were caused by German agents who were
buying the metal there and probably shipping to Germany via Japan
or Russia.
"Mr. Soholtz stated that in his opinion the above
mentioned application was rejected because of his letter. He
also advised that he was still holding the platinum pending in-
structions from Zaher Hermanos for its sale, which, of course,
is apparently being withheld until the price in this country
will rise."

My dear Mr. rr sident:

express to you, further my esteem confidents in you and the action
which you deem necessary to take under present day events abroad
and at home as well.
On May 27th last I set and listened to your radio address
with much interest, and in that address you declared an unlimited
National Emergency for our national defense, sence that date you
have appointed the Honorable Harold L. Ickes, as oil co-ordinator.
Mr. President permit me to be again very plain, in my own
opposition to this transaction as well as many other fellow-
americans espc. my co-workers in the machine tool factory (Largest)
here In Cincinnati. Need I say too you that you know who our
aggressors are to the Atlantic Ocean for you are well aware there-
to. Now in this grave emergency you have milions of fellow ameri
cans who is willing to go hand in hand with you and do without
a number of things for national defense but I say this if you now
let such actions as this acer, you are. going to bring about a much
greator per cent of Disunity with in than we already have not at
all including the strikes in Defense plants.
Will you please give me your answer to these clippings
and the reason whereby we should give up our needs at times to
aid Japan a country that would like to including Hitler attack us.
Adding hereto a suggestion that lesislation be enacted at once to
out law theses strikes even if you have to conscript labor to do
so I feel sure that you would not take away its gains attained under
you and through the Wagner Labor act. Thanking you for an early
answer, I remain,
Most sincerely
Yours truly
3245 E. Fourth St.
GEO. R. ANDERSON
Cincinnati, Ohio

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
I think the fol lowinr infory tion whieh nee cone
to my attention will be of interest t you:
"Preliminary figures indicate total shipment fron California
C
to Japan during May 1941 of around 2.000.000 barre1s, or
roughly 68,000 barrels per day. This compares with an
7
average for the first 4 months of 1941 of about 45,600 B/D
and represents the largest month in shipments to Japan since
1.2
July 1940. However, no undue significance is placed on
5.
this fact which is probably due to coincidence of sailings
and to the fact that three large whaling tankers were made
available during May for the transportation of petroleum.
These three tankers alone account for about 400,000 barrels.
7
A
"So far as can be learned no gasoline licenses have been
7
granted to Japanese in from three to four months and at
least two large suppliers of this comnodity feel that no
further gasoline licenses may be granted, at leas: for the
1:41
time being. This may necessitate some change in Japanese
operations by requiring more select types of crude, with
the emphasis being placed on the gasoline content thereof,
always pre-supposing, of course that licenses will be
granted for crude. So fer as can be ascertained, crude
licenses are still coming through although with some greater
delay than in the past.
"Japanese demand in general is very steady but non-spectacular.
Japanese operations are greatly hampered by shortage of trans-
portation and from general observation the Japanese appear to
be buying to the absolute limit of their ability to tranaport
the olr.This limit appears to be at a geherar average or 
about 50,000 barrels per day, which is about 20% less than
the average of shipments during 1940.
Japan seems to be always in the market for Lubricating Oil
PS/NW
Distillate and for select types of Crude. They appear to
have comparatively little interest at the moment in Diesel
Oil and none at all in Fuel Oil."
Yours very truly,
stward

It will be noted that relatively little material
has been reported in the past few months as having been
shipped to Dairen and the reason given is shortage of trans-
portation. There is, however, of course, no way of knowing
what material listed for shipment to Japan may have been
diverted to Dairen, which might be a convenient way of satis-
fying the inhibition on the part of some suppliers against
shipment to Manchukuo.

There anpears to be a most consistent demand from
Japan for California lubricating oils and lubrica ing oil dis-
tillates and both these commodities have become tighter than
at any time in the past number of years. Not only are the pro-
ducts scarce, but a definite bottleneck has developed in fiiling
finished lubricants into wood barrels. Most of the sunpliers
are not really nroperly set up for this b siness which involves
a much greater time than for filling steel drums. Practically
all sunpliers have actually been forced to turn down some lubri-
cating oil business, not only for Janan but for shinment in

Dear Sir:
In answer to your letter of the ninth, we beg to say
that the writer will be in Washington in all probability
next Wednesday, the 16th of July and will call on you
1f convenient to you, at ten o'clock in the morning.
Should you desire any special information, I would
appreciate it if you would let me know in time so
that it can be properly prepared.
If, by any chance, the appointment at ten o'clock on
Wednesday should not be convenient to you, would you
please leave a message at the Shoreham Hotel in Wash-
ington, Room 307-F (where I will arrive Tuesday night)
and advise me whatever time would be more convenient
to you.
Very truly yours,
Whirh
